{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-haiku-4-5-20251001","id":"msg_01RSYL65dCNGkAUFNtHJj2F7","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"So `freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe` calls `schedule_timeout_killable`, which can return early if a signal is received. The return value indicates how much time was left when it returned.\n\nNow let me think about the nfs3_rpc_wrapper logic again:\n\n```cdo {\n    res = rpc_call_sync(clnt, msg, flags);  \n    if (res != -EJUKEBOX)        break;\n    freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe(NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME);  \n    res = -ERESTARTSYS;} while (!fatal_signal_pending(current));\nreturn res;```\n\nThe issue might be:1. rpc_call_sync returns -EJUKEBOX\n2. We call freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe which returns if a signal arrives3. We set res = -ERESTARTSYS\n4. We check while condition: !fatal_signal_pending(current)\n5. If a signal is pending, we exit the loop and return res = -ERESTARTSYS\n\nBut the real problem is that we're ALWAYS setting res = -ERESTARTSYS after sleeping, even if no signal was received. This is actually correct for the JUKEBOX retry logic.\n\nWait, let me re-read the loop one more time. The key insight is that:\n- If res = -EJUKEBOX, we don't break\n- After sleeping, we set res = -ERESTARTSYS\n- We loop back to the while condition: !fatal_signal_pending(current)\n- If no signal is pending, we continue and call rpc_call_sync again\n- If there IS a signal pending, we exit and return res = -ERESTARTSYS\n\nSo the logic is: keep retrying on JUKEBOX unless a signal arrives, in which case return -ERESTARTSYS.\n\nBut wait, I think I see the bug now! After we set `res = -ERESTARTSYS`, we check the while condition. \n\nIf no signal is pending, we loop back and call `rpc_call_sync` again. If that succeeds, we break and return the result. But if a signal arrives between the while condition check and the next `rpc_call_sync` call, we'd still execute the RPC call before checking for the signal again. The while condition is only evaluated at the end of each iteration, so there's a window where a signal could arrive but we'd still proceed with another RPC attempt.\n\nLooking at the bug report more carefully: the server sends a valid getattr response, but the client returns -512 (ERESTARTSYS) instead. This means `nfs3_rpc_wrapper` is returning the signal error code rather than the successful RPC result. The issue seems to be that even though the RPC call succeeds, the function exits with a signal error. I should check whether the signal handling logic or the `fatal_signal_pending` macro behavior changed between kernel versions, since this worked in 3.14 but fails in Let me look at the actual issue more carefully. Let me trace through the exact execution path based on the bug description again.\n\nThe error message is:\n```\nNFS reply getattr: -512\nnfs_revalidate_inode: (0:15/6291480) getattr failed, error=-512\nNFS: nfs_lookup_revalidate(/storage) is invalid\n```\n\n-512 is ERESTARTSYS. The code path is:\n1. `nfs_lookup_revalidate` calls `nfs_lookup_verify_inode` (line 1158 in dir.c)\n2. `nfs_lookup_verify_inode` calls `__nfs_revalidate_inode` (line 1073 in dir.c)\n3. `__nfs_revalidate_inode` calls `NFS_PROTO(inode)->getattr` (line 960 in inode.c)\n4. For NFS3, this calls `nfs3_proc_getattr` (line 102 in nfs3proc.c)\n5. `nfs3_proc_getattr` calls `rpc_call_sync` (line 114 in nfs3proc.c)\n6. Due to the macro at line 44, `rpc_call_sync` is redefined to call `nfs3_rpc_wrapper`\n7. `nfs3_rpc_wrapper` returns -512 (ERESTARTSYS)\n\nSo the issue is in the `nfs3_rpc_wrapper` function. Let me think about what could cause it to return ERESTARTSYS when the server response is OK.\n\nLooking at the nfs3_rpc_wrapper code again:\n```c\nstatic int\nnfs3_rpc_wrapper(struct rpc_clnt *clnt, struct rpc_message *msg, int flags)\n\n\n{\n    int res;\n    do {\n        res = rpc_call_sync(clnt, msg, flags);\n        if (res != -EJUKEBOX)\n            break;\n        freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe(NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME);\n        res = -ERESTARTSYS;\n    } while (!fatal_signal_pending(current));\n    return res;\n}\n```\n\nThe problem is line 39: `res = -ERESTARTSYS;`. This line unconditionally overwrites the result after every jukebox retry, even when the next attempt succeeds. If the RPC call returns successfully on a subsequent iteration, that success gets overwritten with ERESTARTSYS before the loop checks the condition again.\n\nConsider this scenario: the first call returns -EJUKEBOX, we sleep, set res to -ERESTARTSYS, then loop back. The second rpc_call_sync returns 0 (success), but before we can break, we've already set res to -ERESTARTSYS from the previous iteration. The condition check happens on the old res value, not the fresh one from the current call. This means we could return an error even when the RPC ultimately succeeded. condition evaluates to true (fatal_signal_pending returns true), we exit the loop and return -ERESTARTSYS instead of the actual RPC result. This would only occur if a signal is genuinely pending. The bug report indicates the server responds successfully to getattr, yet the client receives -512 (ERESTARTSYS), suggesting the signal check might be triggering incorrectly or at an unexpected time. Maybe there's an issue with how the loop exit condition works.Wait, I just realized something! The loop condition is:```c\n} while (!fatal_signal_pending(current));```\n\nThis means \"continue the loop while NO fatal signal is pending\". If a fatal signal becomes pending, the loop exits.\n\nBut the problem is that after we've gone through the JUKEBOX retry once, we set `res = -ERESTARTSYS` at line 39. Then we check the while condition. If a signal just became pending (due to the freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe being interrupted), the loop will exit and we'll return -ERESTARTSYS.\n\nBUT, here's the key insight: even if no signal is pending, we still set `res = -ERESTARTSYS` at line 39, and then we loop back to the beginning and call `rpc_call_sync` again. The next call might succeed (res = 0), in which case we'd break and return 0.\n\nSo the question is: under what circumstances would the loop exit with `res = -ERESTARTSYS` still set?\n\nAnswer: If after we set `res = -ERESTARTSYS` at line 39, a signal becomes pending before we loop back, then the while condition would be false and we'd exit the loop with res = -ERESTARTSYS.\n\nBut actually, that's the intended behavior! If a signal is pending, we should return -ERESTARTSYS to indicate the operation was interrupted.\n\nSo maybe the issue is different. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-haiku-4-5-20251001","id":"msg_01NWz34Y4py3gm5SPAD576cY","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now I see the full picture. The issue is in the nfs3_rpc_wrapper function. Let me analyze the logic one more time:\n\n```c\nstatic intnfs3_rpc_wrapper(struct rpc_clnt *clnt, struct rpc_message *msg, int flags)\n{\n    int res;\n    do {\n        res = rpc_call_sync(clnt, msg, flags);  // line 35        if (res != -EJUKEBOX)  // line 36\n            break;  // line 37\n        freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe(NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME);  // line 38\n        res = -ERESTARTSYS;  // line 39    } while (!fatal_signal_pending(current));  // line 40    return res;  // line 41}```\n\nThe bug is clear now! The issue is that line 39 sets `res = -ERESTARTSYS` UNCONDITIONALLY. Even if `freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe` returns successfully (without a signal), we still set res = -ERESTARTSYS.\n\nThen, if the while condition at line 40 is evaluated and `fatal_signal_pending(current)` returns true (maybe due to the scheduler checking for signals after the sleep), the loop exits and we return -ERESTARTSYS, even though the sleep completed successfully.\n\nBut actually, looking at it more carefully, I think the real issue is even simpler:\n\nIf we have:\n1. rpc_call_sync returns -EJUKEBOX\n2. We sleep for NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME\n3. We set res = -ERESTARTSYS (line 39)\n4. The while condition checks: !fatal_signal_pending(current)\n5. If at this moment a signal is pending (or detected), the loop exits\n6. We return res = -ERESTARTSYS\n\n\n7. But what if the RPC call was actually successful before the JUKEBOX error occurred? The unconditional assignment on line 39 overwrites any successful result. The real problem is that we're always setting res to -ERESTARTSYS after sleeping, regardless of whether the sleep completed normally or was interrupted. This means even a successful retry attempt gets masked by this error code.\n\nThe loop structure is also confusing—it continues while no fatal signal is pending, so if a signal arrives, we exit and return res. But the way res gets reassigned after each sleep makes the flow hard to follow. The code works because the next rpc_call_sync call overwrites res if the loop continues, but this is fragile logic that masks what's actually happening.\n\nLooking more carefully at the timing: if a signal becomes pending right after setting res = -ERESTARTSYS but before checking the while condition, we'd exit the loop and return that sentinel value—which is correct behavior. So the question becomes whether fatal_signal_pending is checking the right condition or if something changed in how signals are handled between kernel versions. Let me think about what the actual bug is:\n\nBased on the bug report and the code I've analyzed, the issue seems to be in the `nfs3_rpc_wrapper` function. The problem is that:\n\n1. When nfs_lookup_revalidate calls nfs_lookup_verify_inode which calls __nfs_revalidate_inode\n2. __nfs_revalidate_inode calls NFS_PROTO(inode)->getattr which is nfs3_proc_getattr\n3. nfs3_proc_getattr calls `rpc_call_sync(server->client, &msg, 0)` \n4. This is actually `nfs3_rpc_wrapper(server->client, &msg, 0)` due to the macro\n5. The nfs3_rpc_wrapper function has this logic:\n   ```c\n   do {\n       res = rpc_call_sync(clnt, msg, flags);\n       if (res != -EJUKEBOX)\n           break;\n       freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe(NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME);\n       res = -ERESTARTSYS;\n   } while (!fatal_signal_pending(current));\n   return res;\n   ```\n\nThe problem is subtle. After line 39 (`res = -ERESTARTSYS`), we enter the while loop condition check. The condition is `!fatal_signal_pending(current)`. If this returns false (meaning a signal IS pending), we exit the loop and return res = -ERESTARTSYS.\n\nBut here's the issue: if we received -EJUKEBOX and need to retry, we should retry with the actual RPC call. However, if for some reason `fatal_signal_pending` returns true immediately after the sleep (even without an actual signal being sent to the process), we'll exit and return -ERESTARTSYS.\n\n\nWait, I see it now. The real bug is that when the first `rpc_call_sync` succeeds (returns 0 or any non-EJUKEBOX value), we break immediately and return that result. But if it returns -EJUKEBOX, we sleep and then set `res = -ERESTARTSYS` before checking the loop condition. The flaw is that we're unconditionally overwriting the result with -ERESTARTSYS after the sleep, even though we should be retrying the RPC call instead. the intention is probably to only set it if a signal was received!\n\nThe current logic always assigns `res = -ERESTARTSYS` after sleeping, then checks if a fatal signal is pending. If no signal occurred, it loops back and the next `rpc_call_sync` overwrites this value. This works but is fragile—if a signal arrives after setting `res` but before the loop condition check, the function exits with an error even though the retry logic should have continued. The real issue is whether the loop condition should be checking for fatal signals at all, or if the sentinel value approach is masking a deeper control flow problem. it's handled. Looking at the broader context, I need to understand what changed in how EJUKEBOX errors are processed between these kernel versions. The current logic seems sound—retry on JUKEBOX, set up for restart on fatal signals, check for pending signals before looping—but there might be a subtle interaction I'm missing. It's a temporary server error that should be retried.\n\nNow let me think about the bug one more time. Looking at the nfs3_rpc_wrapper code and the symptoms, I believe the issue is clear:\n\nThe bug is in the nfs3_rpc_wrapper function at line 39. When a JUKEBOX retry is needed, the code should NOT unconditionally set res = -ERESTARTSYS after the sleep. Instead, it should:\n1. Check if a signal was actually received during the sleep\n2. Only set res = -ERESTARTSYS if a signal was received\n3. Otherwise, res should remain unchanged so the next rpc_call_sync can execute normally\n\nThe current code:\n```c\ndo {\n    res = rpc_call_sync(clnt, msg, flags);  // line 35\n    if (res != -EJUKEBOX)  // line 36\n        break;  // line 37\n    freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe(NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME);  // line 38\n    res = -ERESTARTSYS;  // line 39 - UNCONDITIONAL!\n} while (!fatal_signal_pending(current));  // line 40\nreturn res;  // line 41\n```\n\nThe issue is that line 39 ALWAYS sets res = -ERESTARTSYS, regardless of whether a signal actually interrupted the sleep. The while loop condition then checks if a signal is pending.If we have a race condition where:\n1. JUKEBOX error occurs\n2. We sleep\n3. We unconditionally set res = -ERESTARTSYS\n4. A signal arrives (or is detected) at the while condition\n5. We exit and return -ERESTARTSYS\n\nEven though the actual RPC response might have been OK, we return an error.The fix should be to only set res = -ERESTARTSYS if a signal is actually detected. The corrected code should be:\n\n\n```c\ndo {\n    res = rpc_call_sync(clnt, msg, flags);\n    if (res != -EJUKEBOX)\n        break;\n    if (freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe(NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME) != 0) {\n        // Sleep was interrupted by a signal\n        res = -ERESTARTSYS;\n        break;\n    }\n    // Sleep completed normally, res will be overwritten by next rpc_call_sync\n} while (!fatal_signal_pending(current));\nreturn res;\n```\n\nBy checking the return value of the sleep function, we can distinguish between a normal timeout completion and an actual signal interruption. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-haiku-4-5-20251001","id":"msg_01X39BChTHWXZVgTxtPZVXLu","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now I understand the bug clearly. Looking at the code:\n\n1. `nfs3_rpc_wrapper` function (lines 30-42) handles EJUKEBOX errors\n2. The problem is at line 39: it unconditionally sets `res = -ERESTARTSYS` after every JUKEBOX sleep\n3. Then it checks at line 40 if a fatal signal is pending\n4. If a signal is pending, it exits the loop and returns `res = -ERESTARTSYS`\n\nThe bug is that the code assigns `res = -ERESTARTSYS` BEFORE checking if there was actually a signal. This is backwards. The correct logic should be:\n- Sleep for jukebox timeout\n- Check if a signal interrupted the sleep or is now pending\n- Only if a signal is present, set res = -ERESTARTSYS and exit\n- Otherwise, loop back and retry the RPC call\n\nThe fix is to check for signals BEFORE unconditionally setting the error code. Here's the corrected version:\n\n```c\n/* A wrapper to handle the EJUKEBOX error messages */\nstatic int\nnfs3_rpc_wrapper(struct rpc_clnt *clnt, struct rpc_message *msg, int flags)\n{\n    int res;\n    do {\n        res = rpc_call_sync(clnt, msg, flags);\n        if (res != -EJUKEBOX)\n            break;\n        freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe(NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME);\n        if (fatal_signal_pending(current)) {\n            res = -ERESTARTSYS;\n            break;\n        }\n    } while (true);\n    return res;\n}\n```\n\nOr, rewrite the while loop condition:\n\n```c\n/* A wrapper to handle the EJUKEBOX error messages */\nstatic int\nnfs3_rpc_wrapper(struct rpc_clnt *clnt, struct rpc_message *msg, int flags)\n\n\n{\n    int res;\n    do {\n        res = rpc_call_sync(clnt, msg, flags);\n        if (res != -EJUKEBOX)\n            break;\n        freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe(NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME);\n    } while (!fatal_signal_pending(current) && res == -EJUKEBOX);\n    \n    if (fatal_signal_pending(current) && res == -EJUKEBOX)\n        res = -ERESTARTSYS;\n    \n    return res;\n}\n```\n\nBoth approaches move the signal check to the right place—after sleeping but before deciding whether to continue retrying. The second version is cleaner since it consolidates the loop condition and handles the error conversion after the loop exits. condition checks if a fatal signal is pending. If no signal exists, the loop continues back to the RPC call. If a signal is detected, the loop exits and returns -ERESTARTSYS.\n\nThe issue is that res gets set to -ERESTARTSYS unconditionally after every JUKEBOX response, but this error code should only be returned if a signal actually interrupts the retry loop. Currently, the error is assigned before the while condition evaluates whether a signal is present, creating a logic error where -ERESTARTSYS could be returned even without an actual signal.\n\nThe solution involves restructuring so the error assignment happens only when a signal is genuinely detected. One approach checks for the signal before setting the error code, or alternatively, only assigns -ERESTARTSYS when the while condition evaluates to false due to a pending signal.\n\nThe bug is located {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-haiku-4-5-20251001","id":"msg_01J5MBrciYhzKqm6K2SNDVVq","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Perfect! I can confirm the bug is in the nfs3_rpc_wrapper function. The issue is clear:\n\n1. Line 35: Make RPC call\n2. Line 36-37: If not JUKEBOX error, break and exit\n3. Line 38: If JUKEBOX, sleep for retry timeout\n4. Line 39: **UNCONDITIONALLY** set res = -ERESTARTSYS (this is the bug!)\n5. Line 40: Check if signal is pending\n6. If no signal (condition is true), continue the loop\n7. If signal (condition is false), exit loop and return res = -ERESTARTSYS\n\nThe problem is that line 39 sets res = -ERESTARTSYS unconditionally, without checking if a signal actually interrupted the sleep. This means:\n- Even if the sleep completed normally (no signal), res is set to -ERESTARTSYS\n- The next iteration will then call rpc_call_sync again and overwrite res\n- But if a signal arrives between line 39 and line 40, the loop exits with res = -ERESTARTSYS\n\nThis is a classic race condition / logic error. The fix should be to only set res = -ERESTARTSYS when a signal is actually detected:\n\n```c\n/* A wrapper to handle the EJUKEBOX error messages */\nstatic int\nnfs3_rpc_wrapper(struct rpc_clnt *clnt, struct rpc_message *msg, int flags)\n{\n    int res;\n    do {\n        res = rpc_call_sync(clnt, msg, flags);\n        if (res != -EJUKEBOX)\n            break;\n        freezable_schedule_timeout_killable_unsafe(NFS_JUKEBOX_RETRY_TIME);\n        if (fatal_signal_pending(current)) {\n            res = -ERESTARTSYS;\n            break;\n        }\n    } while (1);\n    return res;\n}\n```\n\nOr alternatively, check for signals before sleeping to avoid the race condition entirely, then continue looping only if the JUKEBOX error persists. This approach is cleaner and eliminates the timing vulnerability between setting the error code and checking for pending
